# Run iterators: loops in each function instead of loop macros

Today the loops that walk arrays live in macros: `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()`, `RRAY_BINARY()` and `RRAY_REDUCE()`. Each operation passes in a one-element kernel (`ONE`). Anything an operation needs beyond that one-element kernel, like a 64-bit total or a pairwise sum, has to become a new macro parameter or a new macro. `plans/accuracy.md` shows how quickly that grows.

This plan looks at the alternative: an iterator that the operation drives itself, with the loops written in each function. We built three iterator designs into a variant of rray4 and benchmarked them against the current macros on broadcasting, addition and sums.

---

# Summary

- A per-element iterator (a `next()` that hands back `x` and `out` locations for every element) is simplest to use, but too slow. It's up to 7x slower on sums, 2x to 2.7x slower on double addition, and 1.1x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting.

- An iterator that hands back one run at a time, used with a single loop over the run, matches the macros when every operand moves along the run. When one operand stays fixed, it's 1.3x to 1.5x slower on broadcasting and addition, and 4x to 7x slower on sums.

- The same run iterator, with each function writing one loop per stride case (an operand fixed or moving), matches the macros in nearly every case we measured. The exception is very short runs: adding `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` (a million runs of 10 elements) is 4% to 6% slower, in every one of six runs. It's faster in one case: summing `[10, 1e6]` over axis 1 takes 2.75 ms against 3.53 ms, because the total stays in a local variable.

- That design is what NumPy does. It is also what the macros already do. The loops are the same, so the speed is the same. What changes is where the loops live: in each function, where they can be read and changed, instead of inside a macro.

- One run iterator can serve any number of inputs, replacing today's separate one-input and two-input plans. It's as fast as the specialized ones, as long as each function passes the number of inputs as a literal (`1`, `2`) and the call is inlined. With the count known only at runtime, short runs get 25% to 75% slower.

- The short-run gap goes away when the iterator does less per run: keep one location array that the iterator advances directly (no per-run copy for the caller), and advance at the end of each run instead of checking for the first run at the start, like the macros do. That version is within 2.5% of the macro on every short-run case.

- Keep the plan and the iterator as separate structs. A merged struct is just as fast when it's built inside the kernel. But when the driver builds it and the kernel uses it through a pointer, raw broadcasting gets up to 5x slower and a strided mean 11% slower. With a small `const` plan passed in and the iterator built locally in the kernel, the fast version is the default, with no rule to remember.

- The cost is repetition. rray4 has 84 `RRAY_BINARY()` calls, 32 `RRAY_REDUCE()` calls and 5 `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` calls. Writing every stride case out by hand in each of them is a lot of code. How to handle the per-type repetition is the main open question.

Recommendation: adopt one run iterator for any number of inputs, in the "advance at the end" shape, with per-stride-case loops written in each function. Decide separately how to avoid writing every type out by hand.

---

# How it works today

`src/strided-iterator.h` builds a plan from a point space (the dimensions being walked) and a subspace (the strides of the array being read or written). After merging axes that can be walked as one ("coalescing"), the first axis is walked as one run, and later axes advance between runs with `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT()`.

The header documents three things that matter here:

- "For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions."

- The plan holds only fixed state. The positions that change are kept by the caller, so the compiler can keep them in registers.

- When a stride along the run is 0, the macros take a separate path where the compiler can see the location doesn't change. It then loads the fixed value once and vectorizes the rest.

Each macro owns the full walk: allocate the output, set up the plan, loop over runs, branch on the stride cases, call `ONE` per element, and advance between runs. A few functions (`combine.c`, `index.c`, `permute-axes.c`, `split.c`) already write their own loops against the plan directly, without a macro.

---

# What NumPy does

This section is from knowledge of NumPy's source, not checked against it. NumPy isn't installed on the benchmark machine.

- One iterator, `nditer`, for everything. It reorders axes so the inner loop follows memory order, merges axes that can be walked as one, and hands out one run at a time: a pointer, a length, and a stride for each operand. For reductions, it walks the input in memory order no matter which axis is reduced.

- Reductions reuse the elementwise kernel. `np.add.reduce` calls the same inner loop as `np.add`, with operands `(out, in, out)`. When a whole run feeds one output, the output's stride is 0.

- Each typed inner loop checks its strides and picks a path. The `float64` add loop first checks `IS_BINARY_REDUCE` (output stride 0, output is also the first input). If so, it runs a pairwise sum over the run into one local total. Otherwise it picks a fast path for contiguous operands, a fixed scalar operand, and so on, and uses SIMD there.

- SIMD is written by hand with NumPy's own layer (and Highway for newer kernels), compiled for several CPU levels and picked when NumPy loads. rray4 can only rely on the compiler vectorizing at R's default `-O2`, since CRAN doesn't allow `-march=native`.

- When data needs a type conversion or is misaligned, `nditer` copies it in chunks (8192 elements by default) into a contiguous buffer, and the inner loop runs on that.

- The typed inner loops are generated, not written by hand: once from `.c.src` templates expanded by a Python script, now mostly C++ templates. There is one loop body per operation, expanded across types. The iterator never knows what operation it's running, and the inner loop never knows how many dimensions the array has.

So NumPy uses the "run iterator, per-stride-case loops" design, and avoids writing every type out by generating the per-type loops.

---

# The iterators

Both iterators wrap an existing `rray_strided_iterator_plan` (one subspace) or `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` (two subspaces). They hold the changing positions in a struct on the caller's stack. Because `next()` is `static inline` and the struct never leaves the function, the compiler keeps its fields in registers, so the header's concern about positions in registers doesn't apply. That held for every case below, with Apple clang 17. GCC is not checked.

## Run iterator

`next()` advances one run. The caller gets the run's range in the point space (`start` to `end`) and, for each subspace, where the run starts (`loc`) and how far to move per element (`stride`).

```c
struct rray_run_iterator {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize loc;
  r_ssize stride;

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize run_size;
  r_ssize loc_start;
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan;
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan
) {
  struct rray_run_iterator it;

  it.plan = plan;
  it.size = rray_strided_iterator_plan_size(plan);
  it.run_size = rray_strided_iterator_plan_run_size(plan);
  it.stride = rray_strided_iterator_plan_run_stride(plan);

  it.start = 0;
  it.end = 0;
  it.loc = 0;
  it.loc_start = 0;

  rray_strided_iterator_plan_point_init(plan, it.v_point);

  return it;
}

static inline bool rray_run_iterator_next(struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
  if (it->end == it->size) {
    return false;
  }

  if (it->end != 0) {
    const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan = it->plan;
    RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT(it->loc_start, it->v_point, plan);
  }

  it->start = it->end;
  it->end = it->start + it->run_size;
  it->loc = it->loc_start;

  return true;
}
```

`rray_run_iterator2` is the same, with `loc1`, `loc2`, `stride1` and `stride2`, built on `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` and `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT2()`. The full code is in the appendix.

The meaning of the two spaces depends on the operation, as it does for the plan today:

- Broadcasting and binary operations walk the output. `start` to `end` indexes the output, and `loc` indexes the input.

- Reductions walk the input. `start` to `end` indexes the input, and `loc` indexes the output.

## Element iterator

`next()` advances one element and hands back `index` (in the point space) and `loc` (in the subspace). It starts at `loc = -stride` and `run_i = -1`, so the first call goes through the same "next element in this run" path as every other call, without a special first branch. The full code is in the appendix. It is here as the baseline that was ruled out.

---

# Worked examples

All three use the run iterator with a loop per stride case. These are the `run_split` variants from the benchmarks, exactly as they were compiled.

## Broadcast

One input, walking the output. When the input's stride is 0, the run repeats one value.

```c
static r_obj* rray_broadcast_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan
) {
  const r_ssize size = rray_strided_iterator_plan_size(plan);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_double(size));
  double* v_out = r_dbl_begin(out);

  const double* v_x = r_dbl_cbegin(x);

  struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_run_iterator(plan);

  while (rray_run_iterator_next(&it)) {
    if (it.stride == 0) {
      const double x_elt = v_x[it.loc];

      for (r_ssize i = it.start; i < it.end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = x_elt;
      }
    } else {
      r_ssize x_loc = it.loc;

      for (r_ssize i = it.start; i < it.end; ++i) {
        v_out[i] = v_x[x_loc];
        x_loc += it.stride;
      }
    }
  }

  FREE(1);
  return out;
}
```

For example, broadcasting `[1, 4000]` to `[4000, 4000]` gives 4000 runs of 4000 elements, each with input stride 0. Every run takes the first branch and fills with one value. Broadcasting `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]` gives 4000 runs with input stride 1, each copying the same 4000 contiguous values.

## Add

Two inputs, walking the output. Each input is either fixed or moving along the run, so there are four cases. The integer version passes `error_call` to its one-element kernel directly, with no `RRAY_BINARY_ARGS()` needed.

```c
static r_obj* rray_add_int_int(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* y,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator2_plan* plan,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  const r_ssize size = rray_strided_iterator2_plan_size(plan);

  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_integer(size));

Bash: cat src/strided-iterator2.h src/decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h; sed -n 1,200p src/broadcast.c 
cat src/strided-iterator2.h src/decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h; sed -n 1,200p src/broadcast.c
#ifndef RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR2_H
#define RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR2_H

#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "rlang.h"
#include "size.h"

#include "decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h"

// Strided iterator plan
//
// Optimizes and assists in walking a multidimensional point space defined by
// `v_dimensions`. As it walks, it updates a user provided `start` in an
// alternate subspace defined by `v_strides`.
//
// For performance and flexibility, the user is responsible for managing the run
// loop along the first axis. We've tried many alternative approaches but they
// tend to tank performance quickly as you increase the level of abstractions.
//
// The plan holds only immutable state. The positions that change as it walks
// are managed by the caller. This lets the compiler keep these positions in
// registers, which has significant performance implications.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Examples
//
// For broadcasting, the dimensions you broadcast to make up the larger point
// space. This is walked in order. The original dimensions of the array make
// up the subspace. So as you walk the output's point space you can create
// `location`s back into your original array to pull from.
//
// For reducing, it's actually a special form of broadcasting. The original
// dimensions of the array are the point space. The reduced dimensions are the
// subspace. So as you walk the original array, you can create `location`s into
// the output to accumulate the reduced result at.
//
// For permuting axes, the permuted dimensions make up the point space. The
// original dimensions of the array make up the subspace. So as you walk the
// output's point space you can create `location`s back into your original
// array to pull from.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Optimization - First axis runs
//
// After coalescing, the iterator walks the entire first axis in one inner run
// while holding all later axes fixed. It only updates the later point
// coordinates between runs, rather than checking and carrying them after every
// element. This gives the compiler a small loop where the index and locations
// advance by fixed strides, making it much easier to optimize and vectorize.
//
// Practical examples of when this is useful:
//
// - Identically shaped binary array operations. Adding two [2, 4, 5] arrays
//   coalesces to dimensions [40], so the entire operation is one first axis run
//   where both input locations advance contiguously.
//
// - Broadcasting over a later axis. Broadcasting a [2, 3] array to [2, 3, 4]
//   coalesces to dimensions [6, 4] with location strides [1, 0]. The iterator
//   performs four first axis runs of size 6, copying six contiguous values
//   before updating the later axis.
//
// - Reducing over a later axis. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array over its third axis
//   also coalesces to dimensions [6, 4] with output strides [1, 0]. Each first
//   axis run accumulates one contiguous slice into six output locations before
//   advancing along the reduced axis.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Optimization - Coalescing
//
// Coalescing axes is an important optimization used to reduce the number of
// axes we have to iterate over by "merging" adjacent compatible ones. This can
// improve performance by 3-5x on its own in some cases.
//
// Practical examples of when this is useful:
//
// - Identically shaped binary array operations. Adding two arrays with
//   dimensions [2, 4, 5] gives both inputs strides [1, 2, 8]. All adjacent axes
//   coalesce (2 * 1 = 2, 4 * 2 = 8) into one dimension [40] with stride [1],
//   resulting in one flat vectorizable loop.
//
// - Scalar broadcasting across an entire array. Adding a scalar to a [2, 4, 5]
//   array gives the array strides [1, 2, 8] and the scalar strides [0, 0, 0].
//   Both coalesce completely, producing a [40] loop with array stride [1] and
//   scalar stride [0].
//
// - Contiguous adjacent axes within a broadcast operation. Broadcasting a
//   [2, 3] array over point dimensions [2, 3, 4] produces location strides
//   [1, 2, 0]. The adjacent axes 1 and 2 coalesce (2 * 1 = 2) into size 6,
//   leaving dimensions [6, 4] and strides [1, 0].
//
// - Contiguous regions within a reduction. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array over its
//   third axis maps into a [2, 3] result using location strides [1, 2, 0].
//   Coalescing produces dimensions [6, 4], making it the same as the broadcast
//   example above.
//
// - Point-space dimensions of size 1. Output point dimensions of [1, 3, 4] can
//   have strides [0, 1, 3]. The dimension 1 first axis is absorbed into the
//   dimension 3 second axis, adopting stride 1, after which the dimension 4
//   third axis also coalesces. The result is one dimension [12] with stride
//   [1]. Note that this isn't broadcasting. This is when both input and output
//   have an axis that stays dimension 1, which is somewhat rare.
//
// For iterator2, note that both sets of location strides must be coalescible,
// as coalescing changes the output dimensionality, so it's all or nothing.
//
// --------------------------------------------------------------------------
// Optimization - Fixed zero stride paths
//
// After coalescing, the first axis is walked by the inner loop. A stride of 0
// on this axis means that a subspace location stays fixed while the point space
// moves along it. Broadcasting uses this to reuse an input value. Reducing uses
// it to accumulate into the same output location. A zero stride on a later axis
// does not use this path because later axes advance between inner runs.
//
// When the inner loop receives the stride as a runtime value, the compiler
// can't prove that the location is fixed and falls back to a scalar loop. The
// public iteration macros check for a zero stride and pass a literal 0 to a
// specialized path. This lets the compiler see that the location does not
// change. For binary operations, it can then hoist the fixed load out of the
// loop and vectorize the remaining stride 1 work.
//
// Practical examples of when this is useful:
//
// - Scalar broadcasting across an entire array. Adding a scalar to a [2, 4, 5]
//   array coalesces to point dimensions [40], with array strides [1] and scalar
//   strides [0]. The scalar location stays fixed while the array and output
//   advance contiguously. The same path is used by arithmetic, comparison,
//   equality, and extrema operations.
//
// - Row broadcasting within a matrix. Adding a [1, 4] row to a [2, 4] array
//   gives the array strides [1, 2] and row strides [0, 1]. Each inner run
//   therefore reuses one row value while the array and output advance
//   contiguously.
//
// - Higher dimensional row broadcasting. Adding a [1, 3, 4] array to a
//   [2, 3, 4] array coalesces to dimensions [2, 12], with array strides [1, 2]
//   and broadcast strides [0, 1]. Each of the 12 inner runs uses the fixed
//   path.
//
// - Shared leading dimensions of size 1. Adding [1, 1, 4] to [1, 3, 4]
//   absorbs the shared first axis and produces dimensions [3, 4], with first
//   input strides [0, 1] and second input strides [1, 3].
//
// - Reducing over the first axis. Reducing a [2, 3, 4] array to [1, 3, 4]
//   produces output strides [0, 1, 3], which coalesce to dimensions [2, 12]
//   with output strides [0, 1]. Each inner run accumulates into one fixed
//   output location.
struct rray_run_plan {
  r_ssize size;

  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  int dimensionality;

  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
};

static inline struct rray_run_plan rray_run_plan(
  const int* v_dimensions,
  int dimensionality,
  const r_ssize* v_strides,
  r_ssize n
) {
  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);

  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
    r_stop_internal(
      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
      n,
      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
    );
  }

  struct rray_run_plan plan;

  plan.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality,
    r_lazy_null
  );

  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
    plan.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
  }

  r_memcpy(
    plan.v_strides,
    v_strides,
    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
  );

  plan.dimensionality = rray__run_plan_axes_coalesce(
    plan.v_dimensions,
    plan.v_strides,
    n,
    dimensionality
  );

492:Add then reads like the two-input version, with `it.v_loc[0]` / `it.v_loc[1]` and `it.v_stride[0]` / `it.v_stride[1]` in place of `loc1` / `loc2` and `stride1` / `stride2`:
498:  r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];
499:  r_ssize y_loc = it.v_loc[1];
538:With `RRAY_MAX_INPUTS` raised to 64, so `rray_index()` could use it, `run_n` was unchanged: for example 2.84 ms on the short sum along axis 1 and 6.16 ms on the short broadcast. The compiler still keeps the used slots in registers.
554:- It keeps two location arrays. `v_loc_start` is what `next()` advances, and `v_loc` is a copy made at the start of every run for the caller. The copy only protects against a caller that changes `it.v_loc[i]` while walking the run. No caller does: every loop copies the start into a local (`r_ssize x_loc = it.v_loc[0];`) and moves the local.
560:`rray_run_iterator_n_lean` keeps one array, `v_loc`, which `next()` advances directly. The caller reads it and never writes it. C can't enforce that, since the iterator itself has to change the field. It still has the first-run check.
(eval):1: no matches found: --include=*.c

 static inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {

 }

I added the four accessors, and the broadcast loops no longer read the iterator's fields directly. The compiled src/broadcast.c is exactly the same as before, so speed is unaffected.

What changed

  • src/strided-iterator2.h: added rray_run_iterator_start(), rray_run_iterator_end(), rray_run_iterator_loc(it, i) and rray_run_iterator_stride(it, i). Each is a static inline function that takes a const struct rray_run_iterator* and returns one field. They sit between the rray_run_iterator() constructor and rray_run_iterator_done(). I didn't add them to the decl header, since they're defined before anything uses them.

  • src/broadcast.c: both RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC() and RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER() now call the accessors, with i hardcoded to 0 because broadcasting has only one input.

Checks

  • I compiled src/broadcast.c at -O2 with clang, before and after the change, and diffed the disassembly. It's the same instruction for instruction (1165 lines, 6 functions). This is the check that matters for plans/iterators.md: the iterator was already passed by address to done() and next(), and the accessors inline down to the same field loads.

  • The broadcast tests pass (108 of 108). I ran clang-format over all of src/.

Nothing is committed.

Davis

  rray_strided_iterator_plan_point_init(plan, v_point);                        \
                                                                               \
  while (run_start != size) {                                                  \
    const r_ssize run_end = run_start + run_size;                              \
    r_ssize x_loc = x_start;                                                   \
                                                                               \
    for (r_ssize i = run_start; i < run_end; ++i) {                            \
      POKE(out, i, v_x[x_loc]);                                                \
      x_loc += x_run_stride;                                                   \
    }                                                                          \
                                                                               \
    run_start = run_end;                                                       \
    RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT(x_start, v_point, plan);                        \
  }

static void rray_split_fill_lgl(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* out,
  r_ssize x_start,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan
) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_FILL_ATOMIC(int, r_lgl_cbegin, r_lgl_begin);
}

  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* out,
  r_ssize x_start,
  const struct rray_strided_iterator_plan* plan
) {
  RRAY_SPLIT_FILL_ATOMIC(int, r_int_cbegin, r_int_begin);
}

static void rray_split_fill_dbl(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* out,
}

#define RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_LOOP(CTYPE, POKE)                                  \
  const r_ssize group_size = block_size * axis_dimension;                      \
                                                                               \
  r_ssize out_i = 0;                                                           \
                                                                               \
  for (r_ssize group = 0; group < n_groups; ++group) {                         \
    CTYPE const* v_x_group = v_x + group * group_size;                         \
    const int* v_n_group = v_n + group * block_size;                           \
                                                                               \
    for (int j = 0; j < axis_dimension; ++j) {                                 \
      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < block_size; ++i) {                               \
        const int n = v_n_group[i];                                            \
        const int source = (j >= n) ? j - n : j - n + axis_dimension;          \
        POKE(out, out_i, v_x_group[source * block_size + i]);                  \
        ++out_i;                                                               \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
  }

#define RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE) v_out[I] = (VALUE)

#define RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_ATOMIC(CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, DEREF)                  \
  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                           \
  CTYPE* v_out = DEREF(out);                                                   \
                                                                               \
  RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_LOOP(CTYPE, RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_ATOMIC_POKE);

#define RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_BARRIER(CONST_DEREF, POKE)                         \
  r_obj* const* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                          \
                                                                               \
  RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_LOOP(r_obj*, POKE);

static void rray_roll_each_fill_lgl(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* out,
  const int* v_n,
  r_ssize block_size,
  int axis_dimension,
  r_ssize n_groups
) {
  RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_ATOMIC(int, r_lgl_cbegin, r_lgl_begin);
}

static void rray_roll_each_fill_int(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* out,

  r_ssize block_size,
  int axis_dimension,
  r_ssize n_groups

}

#undef RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_LOOP
#undef RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_ATOMIC_POKE
#undef RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_ATOMIC
#undef RRAY_ROLL_EACH_FILL_BARRIER
  }

  FREE(5);
  return out;
}

The loop now lives in one macro, RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(CTYPE, POKE), in src/broadcast.c. The compiled code is exactly the same as the last commit, and nothing is committed yet.

How it's split (same pattern as src/roll-each.c)

  • RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(CTYPE, POKE): the run iterator loop, with both the fixed-stride path and the moving-stride path.

  • RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE): writes v_out[I] = (VALUE).

  • RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC(): allocates out, sets up v_out and v_x, then runs the loop with the atomic poke.

  • RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER(): allocates out, sets up v_x, then runs the loop with the POKE it's given (r_chr_poke() or r_list_poke()).

  • I added all four macros to the #undef list at the end.

In the fixed-stride path, the single value is now declared as CTYPE const x_elt. For the barrier types that expands to r_obj* const, as before, and it matches the CTYPE const* style in src/roll-each.c.

Checks

  • I compiled src/broadcast.c at -O2, before and after, and diffed the disassembly: it's identical.

  • The broadcast tests pass (108 of 108). I ran clang-format over all of src/.

23:- Keep the plan and the iterator as separate structs. A merged struct is just as fast when it's built inside the kernel. With a small `const` plan passed in and the iterator built locally in the kernel, the fast version is the default, with no rule to remember.

# Plan and iterator: separate or merged

Every iterator above keeps two structs: an immutable plan (dimensions, strides, size), built once by the driver and passed to the kernel as a `const` pointer, and an iterator holding the changing positions, built inside the kernel. This section checks whether that split matters, or whether one merged struct would do.

## How the variant package is wired

- Every timing goes through the real entry points (`rray_broadcast()`, `rray_add()`, `rray_sum()`, `rray_mean()`) and their unchanged drivers (`rray_broadcast()` itself, `rray_binary_arithmetic()`, `rray_reduce()`, `rray_reduce_nested()`).

- Each driver already builds the plan, picks the per-type kernel, and calls it through a function pointer with `const plan*`. Every iterator version above is built inside that kernel, as a local, from the plan pointer it already receives. Its address only goes to `static inline` functions (`done()`, `next()`), so it never leaves the kernel, and the compiler can keep its positions in registers. No extra parameters needed threading through.

## The merged struct

```c
struct rray_run_iterator_m {
  r_ssize start;
  r_ssize end;
  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  r_ssize v_stride[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];

  r_ssize size;
  r_ssize run_size;
  int dimensionality;
  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
};

static inline void rray_run_iterator_m_reset(
  struct rray_run_iterator_m* it,
  const r_ssize n
) {
  it->start = 0;
  it->end = it->run_size;

  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
    it->v_loc[i] = 0;
  }

  r_memset(it->v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it->dimensionality);
}
```

`rray_run_iterator_m_init()` copies the dimensions and strides from a plan, then calls `reset()`. `done()` and `next()` are the same as the `advance` iterator, with the plan fields read from the struct itself. With room for 64 dimensions and 64 inputs, the stride array alone is 32 KB.

## Round 1: merged, built inside the kernel

The kernel builds the merged struct itself, copying from the plan the driver passed in. For the nested integer mean (one inner walk per output), three ways to handle the inner walk:

- `advance`: a new separate-plan iterator for each output.

- `merged_reset`: one merged iterator, reset for each output.

- `merged_fresh`: a new merged iterator for each output, copying the plan every time.

The integer mean makes a single pass, so the per-walk setup cost isn't hidden behind a second pass. Median of 3 rounds of 30 iterations, with the order of the versions rotated between rounds.

| Case | Macro | Advance | Merged |
|---|---|---|---|
| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.10 | 7.10 | 7.01 |
| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 6.84 | 7.23 | 7.13 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.38 | 8.40 | 8.52 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 7.89 | 7.88 | 7.95 |
| Add, integer, `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 12.00 | 11.84 | 12.00 |
| Add `[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]` | 10.89 | 10.54 | 10.71 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.68 | 2.88 | 3.05 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 4.01 | 3.92 | 3.98 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 13.80 | 13.80 | 13.78 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 2.44 | 2.42 | 2.40 |

| Integer mean | Outputs | Macro | Advance | Merged, reset | Merged, fresh |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 4000 | 14.16 | 14.17 | 14.15 | 14.15 |
| `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 4000 | 43.25 | 42.84 | 41.65 | 43.01 |
| `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 1e6 | 8.17 | 7.95 | 7.76 | 8.45 |
| `[1e6, 10]` over 2 | 1e6 | 8.16 | 7.91 | 7.87 | 8.51 |
| `[200, 200, 200]` over 1, 3 | 200 | 7.60 | 7.58 | 7.73 | 7.63 |
| `[10, 1e5, 10]` over 1, 3 | 1e5 | 6.61 | 6.50 | 6.54 | 6.64 |

- For a single walk, merged and separate are the same within this session's noise (about 3%).

- Copying the plan for every walk costs 4% to 7% when there are a million outputs. Resetting one merged iterator avoids that copy and matches the separate plan.

This round only shows that merging costs nothing inside one function. A real merged design wouldn't build the struct in the kernel, though. The driver would build it and hand it over.

## Round 2: merged, built by the driver

The drivers build the merged struct and pass a pointer to the kernel. The kernels live in their own file and are marked `noinline`, matching today's function-pointer call from driver to kernel. Three kernel versions:

- `copy`: copies the struct into a local (`struct rray_run_iterator_m it_local = *p_it;`), then runs the same loops as round 1.

- `pointer`: uses the driver's struct through the pointer, with the same loop text (`it->end`, `it->v_stride[0]`, ...).

- `pointer_locals`: uses the pointer, but reads `start`, `end`, the strides and the starting locations into locals at the top of each run.

"Local" is round 1's merged struct built inside the kernel. Same timing method as round 1.

| Raw broadcast | Macro | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 3.35 | 2.86 | 5.47 | 3.16 |
| `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 3.44 | 3.22 | 4.80 | 3.13 |
| `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]` | 1.23 | 1.25 | 6.46 | 1.43 |

| Integer mean | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 14.12 | 14.15 | 14.15 | 14.15 | 14.16 |
| `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 42.77 | 42.82 | 43.00 | 47.71 | 43.12 |
| `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 7.92 | 7.88 | 8.44 | 7.26 | 6.99 |
| `[1e6, 10]` over 2 | 7.87 | 7.87 | 8.45 | 7.26 | 6.98 |
| `[200, 200, 200]` over 1, 3 | 7.67 | 7.59 | 7.93 | 7.60 | 7.64 |
| `[10, 1e5, 10]` over 1, 3 | 6.60 | 6.51 | 7.17 | 7.20 | 7.09 |

| Double | Macro | Local | Copy | Pointer | Pointer, locals |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Broadcast `[10, 1]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.12 | 7.30 | 6.79 | 6.87 | 7.16 |
| Broadcast `[1, 1e6]` to `[10, 1e6]` | 7.15 | 7.16 | 7.02 | 7.14 | 7.03 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]` | 8.24 | 8.49 | 8.25 | 8.44 | 8.67 |
| Add `[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]` | 8.04 | 7.88 | 7.73 | 7.71 | 7.65 |
| Add `[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]` | 10.63 | 10.90 | 10.61 | 10.48 | 10.70 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 1 | 3.90 | 2.76 | 3.60 | 2.89 | 2.72 |
| Sum `[10, 1e6]` over 2 | 3.99 | 3.94 | 3.93 | 3.99 | 3.92 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 1 | 13.78 | 13.78 | 13.76 | 13.78 | 13.79 |
| Sum `[4000, 4000]` over 2 | 2.41 | 2.39 | 2.41 | 2.43 | 2.42 |

## What the numbers say

- Working through the pointer is the trap. For raw output, C allows a byte written through `Rbyte*` to change any object, including `it->end` and `it->v_stride[0]`. So the compiler reloads them on every element and can't vectorize: 5.2x slower on `[4000, 1]` to `[4000, 4000]`. Double output isn't affected, because C's type rules let the compiler assume a `double` write can't change an `r_ssize` field. That's why it never showed up in the double tests. The strided mean also lost 11% through the pointer.

- Reading each run's fields into locals fixes it everywhere, and was the fastest version on the short-walk means (6.98 ms against 7.87 ms for the macro). But it's a rule every loop has to follow. Forgetting it costs nothing visible on `double` and only bites on raw, or anything else written through a character type.

- Copying the struct into a local also avoids the raw problem, but was 30% slower on the short sum and 7% to 10% slower on the short-walk means. The copy happens once per call (about 33 KB, microseconds), so it can't account for milliseconds by itself. A guess is that clang optimizes the loops less well after copying such a large struct. It hasn't been looked into.

- The separate plan avoids all of this without a rule. The kernel gets a small `const` plan, which is only read between runs, and builds its changing positions as locals that can't be confused with the output.

So keep the plan and the iterator separate. The reason isn't that merging is slower in itself. It's that the split makes the fast version the default. The header comment in `src/strided-iterator.h` should give that reason instead of "positions managed by the caller stay in registers", since round 1 shows an iterator struct keeps them in registers just as well.

For the nested reducer, the inner iterator should get a `reset()` that puts it back at the start of the plan, with a new starting location, instead of building a new iterator for every output.

---

# The cost: repetition

Moving the loops into each function means each function carries its own copy of the stride cases. Today:

| Macro | Call sites | Stride cases per call |
|---|---|---|
| `RRAY_BINARY()` | 84 | 4 |
| `RRAY_REDUCE()` | 32 | 2 |
| `RRAY_REDUCE_OUTER()` | 6 | 1 (plus an inner loop) |
| `RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC()` | 5 | 2 |
| `RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER()` | 2 | 2 |

The add example above is about 50 lines. Written out by hand, 84 binary call sites is several thousand lines that differ only in types and the one-element kernel. Most of those call sites differ only by type: `rray_add_dbl_dbl()`, `rray_add_int_dbl()`, `rray_add_lgl_dbl()` and so on.

Options for the per-type repetition:

- Write every type out. Every loop is plain C you can read. The most code, and a fix to one loop has to be copied to the others.

- One loop body per operation, stamped out per type by a small macro. This is what NumPy does with templates. The macro would only fill in types and the kernel, not hide the loop structure: the loops for `add` would be written once in `arithmetic-add.c`, not in a shared header. Different operations can still choose different loops, which is the point.

- A mix: write out the operations with special needs (sums, means, anything in `plans/accuracy.md`), and keep a type-stamping macro for the many plain elementwise operations (arithmetic, comparison, equality).

---

# Open questions

- How should the per-type repetition be handled? Write it out, stamp it per type, or a mix?

- Should checked integer operations skip the fixed-operand cases, given they were slower there?

- Should the run iterators replace direct uses of `RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT()` in `combine.c`, `index.c`, `permute-axes.c` and `split.c` too, so there's one way to walk an array?

- Should `rray_strided_iterator_plan` and `rray_strided_iterator2_plan` be replaced by `rray_strided_iterator_n_plan` and `rray_run_iterator_n`, so there is one plan and one iterator?

- The `advance` shape needs two calls (`done()` and `next()`) and a `for` loop instead of one `while (next())`. Is that acceptable, or is there a single-call shape that is just as fast?

- None of this has been measured with GCC or on x86_64. Is one Linux run worth doing before committing to the design?

---

# Appendix

I think it can work now, and I'd try it. The plan doc already supports it: a single struct is as fast as two, as long as the typed kernel builds it as a local variable. What failed was the driver building it and handing the kernel a pointer.

What plans/iterators.md already shows

  • Merged struct built inside the kernel (round 1): within noise of the separate plan for broadcast, add, sum and mean. That is your proposal, except round 1 still copied from a plan, and you would drop the plan.

  • Merged struct built by the driver (round 2): used through a pointer, raw broadcast was up to 5.2x slower. A write through Rbyte* may legally change any object, so the compiler reloads end and the stride on every element. A local whose address never leaves the function can't be changed that way. That's why building the struct in the kernel avoids the problem.

What would change

  • rray_run_iterator takes in all n strides (axis-major), plus v_dimensions, dimensionality and size. v_stride goes away: the first-axis strides are just row 0 of v_strides.

  • The accessors we just added keep call sites the same. rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, i) would read it.v_strides[i], so the loop macros don't need to change.

  • Each typed kernel takes raw arguments, something like (x, v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides), and builds the iterator itself, coalescing included. It gets the output size from the iterator.

Risks I'd check before trusting it

  1. Where the build code goes. If the constructor is static inline, the coalescing loop is copied into every typed kernel. That's 7 for broadcast today, but over 100 once binary and reduce move over, so the package binary grows. If it's a normal function that fills struct rray_run_iterator* it, the iterator's address leaves the kernel, and the raw slowdown from round 2 could come back. A normal function that returns the struct by value might avoid both problems, but that needs checking in the disassembly, not assuming.

  2. Raw broadcast was never measured with a local merged struct. Round 2 measured raw broadcast through a pointer or a copy only, and round 1 used double only. [4000, 1] to [4000, 4000] in raw is the case that matters most here.

  3. The copy slowdown was never explained. Copying the merged struct into a local was 30% slower on the short sum, even though the copy itself takes microseconds. The guess was that clang optimizes less well around a struct that large. With RRAY_MAX_INPUTS now equal to RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY, the stride array is 64 × 64 × 8 bytes = 32 KB. That's the same size that caused trouble.

  4. Nested reducers. Building a new iterator for every output cost 4% to 7% with a million outputs. With one struct, that would also redo the coalescing every time. These reducers would need a reset() that moves the iterator back to the start with a new starting location. Round 1's merged_reset matched the separate plan. This PR doesn't touch nested reducers, but the design has to allow for it.

How I'd check speed

  • Disassembly: look at the raw and double broadcast inner loops after the change. Check that end and the stride stay in registers, and that the raw fixed-stride loop is vectorized. This is the fast, reliable check for risks 1 and 2.

  • Benchmarks: rerun the broadcast cases from the plan's benchmark script against the current commit. Include [10, 1] to [10, 1e6], [1, 1e6] to [10, 1e6] and [4000, 1] to [4000, 4000], for both raw and double. Take medians over several rounds, rotating the order of the versions between rounds.

  • Constructor options: run the checks for both versions from risk 1 (inline, and a normal function that returns by value), and compare binary size too.

  • Struct size: try a smaller RRAY_MAX_INPUTS once to see whether risk 3 shows up.

  • The rest of the matrix: add, sum and nested mean only matter once those operations move over, so I'd rerun them in that PR rather than this one.

Broadcast is the only user in this PR, so the experiment is cheap. Before I start, two questions:

  • Should I try it on this branch, or on a separate scratch branch first?

  • Do you want me to write it up as a new section in plans/iterators.md with the numbers, like the earlier rounds?

Davis

Try it on this branch, heavily benchmark it against all these cases you mention

## Benchmark script

Run from the root of the variant package. This is the exact script behind the tables. For "Any number of inputs", the `variants` line was changed to `c(macro = 0L, run_split = 3L, run_n = 4L, run_n_runtime = 5L)`, and nothing else.

```r
devtools::load_all(quiet = TRUE)

variants <- c(macro = 0L, element = 1L, run = 2L, run_split = 3L)

set_variant <- function(variant) {
  invisible(.Call(ffi_rray_set_iterator_variant, variant))
}

set.seed(1)

dbl <- function(...) array(runif(prod(c(...))), c(...))
int <- function(...) array(sample(100L, prod(c(...)), TRUE), c(...))

m <- dbl(4000, 4000)
cube <- dbl(200, 200, 200)
wide <- dbl(10, 1e6)

mi <- int(4000, 4000)
wide_i <- int(10, 1e6)

cases <- list(
  broadcast = list(
    "[4000, 1] to [4000, 4000]" = local({
      x <- dbl(4000, 1)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(4000L, 4000L))
    }),
    "[1, 4000] to [4000, 4000]" = local({
      x <- dbl(1, 4000)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(4000L, 4000L))
    }),
    "[1, 1] to [4000, 4000]" = local({
      x <- dbl(1, 1)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(4000L, 4000L))
    }),
    "[200, 1, 200] to [200, 200, 200]" = local({
      x <- dbl(200, 1, 200)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(200L, 200L, 200L))
    }),
    "[1, 200, 200] to [200, 200, 200]" = local({
      x <- dbl(1, 200, 200)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(200L, 200L, 200L))
    }),
    "[10, 1] to [10, 1e6]" = local({
      x <- dbl(10, 1)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(10L, 1000000L))
    }),
    "[1, 1e6] to [10, 1e6]" = local({
      x <- dbl(1, 1e6)
      \() rray_broadcast(x, c(10L, 1000000L))
    })
  ),
  add_dbl = list(
    "[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]" = local({
      y <- dbl(4000, 4000)
      \() rray_add(m, y)
    }),
    "[4000, 4000] + [4000, 1]" = local({
      y <- dbl(4000, 1)
      \() rray_add(m, y)
    }),
    "[4000, 4000] + [1, 4000]" = local({
      y <- dbl(1, 4000)
      \() rray_add(m, y)
    }),
    "[4000, 4000] + [1, 1]" = local({
      y <- dbl(1, 1)
      \() rray_add(m, y)
    }),
    "[4000, 1] + [1, 4000]" = local({
      x <- dbl(4000, 1)
      y <- dbl(1, 4000)
      \() rray_add(x, y)
    }),
    "[200, 200, 200] + [1, 200, 1]" = local({
      y <- dbl(1, 200, 1)
      \() rray_add(cube, y)
    }),
    "[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]" = local({
      y <- dbl(10, 1)
      \() rray_add(wide, y)
    }),
    "[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]" = local({
      y <- dbl(1, 1e6)
      \() rray_add(wide, y)
    })
  ),
  add_int = list(
    "[4000, 4000] + [4000, 4000]" = local({
      y <- int(4000, 4000)
      \() rray_add(mi, y)
    }),
    "[4000, 4000] + [4000, 1]" = local({
      y <- int(4000, 1)
      \() rray_add(mi, y)
    }),
    "[4000, 4000] + [1, 4000]" = local({
      y <- int(1, 4000)
      \() rray_add(mi, y)
    }),
    "[10, 1e6] + [10, 1]" = local({
      y <- int(10, 1)
      \() rray_add(wide_i, y)
    }),
    "[10, 1e6] + [1, 1e6]" = local({
      y <- int(1, 1e6)
      \() rray_add(wide_i, y)
    })
  ),
  sum = list(
    "[4000, 4000] over 1" = \() rray_sum(m, 1L),
    "[4000, 4000] over 2" = \() rray_sum(m, 2L),
    "[4000, 4000] over 1, 2" = \() rray_sum(m, 1:2),
    "[200, 200, 200] over 1" = \() rray_sum(cube, 1L),
    "[200, 200, 200] over 2" = \() rray_sum(cube, 2L),
    "[200, 200, 200] over 3" = \() rray_sum(cube, 3L),
    "[200, 200, 200] over 1, 3" = \() rray_sum(cube, c(1L, 3L)),
    "[10, 1e6] over 1" = \() rray_sum(wide, 1L),
    "[10, 1e6] over 2" = \() rray_sum(wide, 2L)
  )
)

for (op in names(cases)) {
  for (case in names(cases[[op]])) {
    f <- cases[[op]][[case]]
    set.seed(2)
    set_variant(0L)
    expected <- f()
    for (variant in variants) {
      set.seed(2)
      set_variant(variant)
      stopifnot(identical(f(), expected))
    }
  }

220	  const struct rray_run_plan* plan,
221	  r_ssize n
222	) {
223	  struct rray_run_iterator it;
224	
225	  it.size = plan->size;
226

233	  }

-static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_start(
+struct rray_run_iterator {
+  r_ssize start;
+  r_ssize end;
+  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+
+  r_ssize size;
+  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+
+  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
+  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
+  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
+  int dimensionality;
+
+  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
+  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
+  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
+};
+
+static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
+  const int* v_dimensions,
+  int dimensionality,
+  const r_ssize* v_strides,
+  r_ssize n
+) {
+  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);
+
+  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
+    r_stop_internal(
+      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
+      n,
+      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
+    );
+  }
+
+  struct rray_run_iterator it;
+
+  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
+    v_dimensions,
+    dimensionality,
+    r_lazy_null
+  );
+
+  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
+    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
+  }
+
+  r_memcpy(
+    it.v_strides,
+    v_strides,
+    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
+  );
+
+  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
+    it.v_dimensions,
+    it.v_strides,
+    n,
+    dimensionality
+  );
+
+  it.start = 0;
+  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];
+
+  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
+    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
+  }
+
+  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);
+
+  return it;
+}
+
+static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(
+  const struct rray_run_iterator* it
+) {
+  return it->size;
+}

251	static inline bool rray_run_iterator_done(const struct rray_run_iterator* it) {
252	  return it->start == it->size;

254	
255	static inline void rray_run_iterator_next(
256	  struct rray_run_iterator* it,
257	  const struct rray_run_plan* plan,
258	  r_ssize n
259	) {
260	  for (int axis = 1; axis < plan->dimensionality; ++axis) {
261	    const r_ssize* v_strides = plan->v_strides + axis * n;
262	    const r_ssize dimension = plan->v_dimensions[axis];
263	
264	    ++it->v_point[axis];
265	
266	    if (it->v_point[axis] < dimension) {
267	      for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
268	        it->v_loc[i] += v_strides[i];
269	      }
270	      break;
271	    }
272	
273	    it->v_point[axis] = 0;
274	
275	    for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
276	      it->v_loc[i] -= (dimension - 1) * v_strides[i];
277	    }
278	  }
279	
280	  it->start = it->end;
281	  it->end += plan->v_dimensions[0];
282	}

293	    const r_ssize left_dimension = v_dimensions[out_axis];
294	    r_ssize* v_left_strides = v_strides + out_axis * n;

116	#define RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(CTYPE, POKE)                                       \
117	  for (struct rray_run_iterator it = rray_run_iterator(plan, 1);               \
118	       !rray_run_iterator_done(&it);                                           \
119	       rray_run_iterator_next(&it, plan, 1)) {                                 \
120	    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);                        \
121	    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);                            \
122	                                                                               \
123	    r_ssize x_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);                             \
124	    const r_ssize x_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);                 \
125	                                                                               \
126	    if (x_stride == 0) {                                                       \
127	      CTYPE const x_elt = v_x[x_loc];                                          \
128	      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
129	        POKE(out, i, x_elt);                                                   \
130	      }                                                                        \
131	    } else {                                                                   \
132	      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
133	        POKE(out, i, v_x[x_loc]);                                              \
134	        x_loc += x_stride;                                                     \
135	      }                                                                        \
136	    }                                                                          \
137	  }
138	
139	#define RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE) v_out[I] = (VALUE)
140	
141	#define RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC(RTYPE, CTYPE, CONST_DEREF, DEREF)                \
142	  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(RTYPE, rray_run_plan_size(plan)));          \
143	  CTYPE* v_out = DEREF(out);                                                   \
144	                                                                               \
145	  const CTYPE* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                           \
146	                                                                               \
147	  RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(CTYPE, RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC_POKE);                      \
148	                                                                               \
149	  FREE(1);                                                                     \
150	  return out;
151	
152	#define RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER(RTYPE, CONST_DEREF, POKE)                       \
153	  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(RTYPE, rray_run_plan_size(plan)));          \
154	                                                                               \
155	  r_obj* const* v_x = CONST_DEREF(x);                                          \
156	                                                                               \
157	  RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(r_obj*, POKE);                                           \
158	                                                                               \
159	  FREE(1);                                                                     \
160	  return out;

Read /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/f7982187-88c2-42f0-9599-3410a64631… 
145	//   produces output strides [0, 1, 3], which coalesce to dimensions [2, 12]
146	//   with output strides [0, 1]. Each inner run accumulates into one fixed
147	//   output location.
148	struct rray_run_iterator {
149	  r_ssize start;
150	  r_ssize end;
151	  r_ssize v_loc[RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
152	
153	  r_ssize size;
154	  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
155	
156	  // Since coalescing can multiply two axes' dimensions together, we use an
157	  // `r_ssize` here even though an individual dimension can't be above an `int`.
158	  r_ssize v_dimensions[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];
159	  int dimensionality;
160	
161	  // Strides for all `n` arrays, laid out axis-major in a flat array as
162	  // [dimensionality][n], allowing contiguous access when doing "next" calls.
163	  r_ssize v_strides[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY * RRAY_MAX_INPUTS];
164	};
165	
166	static inline struct rray_run_iterator rray_run_iterator(
167	  const int* v_dimensions,
168	  int dimensionality,
169	  const r_ssize* v_strides,
170	  r_ssize n
171	) {
172	  check_dimensionality(dimensionality);
173	
174	  if (n < 1 || n > RRAY_MAX_INPUTS) {
175	    r_stop_internal(
176	      "`n` (%" R_PRI_SSIZE ") must be between 1 and %d.",
177	      n,
178	      RRAY_MAX_INPUTS
179	    );
180	  }
181	
182	  struct rray_run_iterator it;
183	
184	  it.size = rray_size_from_dimensions_checked(
185	    v_dimensions,
186	    dimensionality,
187	    r_lazy_null
188	  );
189	
190	  for (int axis = 0; axis < dimensionality; ++axis) {
191	    it.v_dimensions[axis] = (r_ssize) v_dimensions[axis];
192	  }
193	
194	  r_memcpy(
195	    it.v_strides,
196	    v_strides,
197	    sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) (dimensionality * n)
198	  );
199	
200	  it.dimensionality = rray__run_iterator_axes_coalesce(
201	    it.v_dimensions,
202	    it.v_strides,
203	    n,
204	    dimensionality
205	  );
206	
207	  it.start = 0;
208	  it.end = it.v_dimensions[0];
209	
210	  for (r_ssize i = 0; i < n; ++i) {
211	    it.v_loc[i] = 0;
212	  }
213	
214	  r_memset(it.v_point, 0, sizeof(r_ssize) * (size_t) it.dimensionality);
215	
216	  return it;
217	}
218	
219	static inline r_ssize rray_run_iterator_size(

I noticed clang inlines all 7 kernels into rray_broadcast() anyway, so the "kernel builds it" case hasn't really been tested since future binary/reduce code calls kernels via function pointers that can't inline. I'll add two more versions, base_nk and inline_nk, with kernels marked noinline to properly cover that case.

Thinking

I found that inline_nk reloads loop state from the stack per run while base_nk keeps it in registers, a difference that matters mainly on short runs, plus the 32 KB frame adds a stack probe on every call. I've added short-run and tiny-array benchmark cases to capture both effects, and now I'm writing the benchmark scripts.

Write /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/f7982187-88c2-42f0-9599-3410a6463… 
args <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)
lib <- args[[1]]
label <- args[[2]]
out_path <- args[[3]]

library(rray4, lib.loc = lib)

set.seed(1)

make <- function(type, dims) {
  n <- prod(dims)
  values <- switch(
    type,
    dbl = runif(n),
    int = sample(100L, n, TRUE),
    raw = as.raw(sample(0:255, n, TRUE)),
    chr = sample(letters, n, TRUE)
  )
  array(values, dims)
}

shapes <- list(
  "[4000, 1] to [4000, 4000]" = list(c(4000, 1), c(4000L, 4000L)),
  "[1, 4000] to [4000, 4000]" = list(c(1, 4000), c(4000L, 4000L)),
  "[1, 1] to [4000, 4000]" = list(c(1, 1), c(4000L, 4000L)),
  "[200, 1, 200] to [200, 200, 200]" = list(c(200, 1, 200), c(200L, 200L, 200L)),
  "[1, 200, 200] to [200, 200, 200]" = list(c(1, 200, 200), c(200L, 200L, 200L)),
  "[10, 1] to [10, 1e6]" = list(c(10, 1), c(10L, 1000000L)),
  "[1, 1e6] to [10, 1e6]" = list(c(1, 1e6), c(10L, 1000000L)),
  "[2, 1] to [2, 5e6]" = list(c(2, 1), c(2L, 5000000L)),
  "[2, 1] x 10 to [2] x 20" = list(rep(c(2, 1), 10), rep(2L, 20))
)

types <- c("dbl", "int", "raw")

cases <- list()

for (type in types) {
  for (shape in names(shapes)) {
    x <- make(type, shapes[[shape]][[1]])
    dimensions <- shapes[[shape]][[2]]
    cases[[paste(type, shape)]] <- local({
      x <- x
      dimensions <- dimensions
      \() rray_broadcast(x, dimensions)
    })
  }
}

for (shape in names(shapes)[c(1, 6)]) {
  x <- make("chr", shapes[[shape]][[1]])
  dimensions <- shapes[[shape]][[2]]
  cases[[paste("chr", shape)]] <- local({
    x <- x
    dimensions <- dimensions
    \() rray_broadcast(x, dimensions)
  })
}

tiny <- list(
  "tiny dbl [2, 1] to [2, 3]" = list(make("dbl", c(2, 1)), c(2L, 3L)),
  "tiny dbl [1] x 5 to [2] x 5" = list(make("dbl", rep(1, 5)), rep(2L, 5))
)

for (name in names(tiny)) {
  cases[[name]] <- local({
    x <- tiny[[name]][[1]]
    dimensions <- tiny[[name]][[2]]
    \() rray_broadcast(x, dimensions)
  })
}

hashes <- vapply(cases, \(f) rlang::hash(f()), character(1))

times <- vapply(
  names(cases),
  function(name) {
    f <- cases[[name]]
    tiny <- startsWith(name, "tiny")
    gc()
    result <- bench::mark(
      f(),
      iterations = if (tiny) 20000 else 25,
      check = FALSE,
      filter_gc = FALSE,
      memory = FALSE
    )
    as.numeric(result$median) * if (tiny) 1e6 else 1e3
  },
  numeric(1)
)

saveRDS(list(label = label, times = times, hashes = hashes), out_path)

args <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)
root <- args[[1]]
rounds <- as.integer(args[[2]])
variants <- strsplit(args[[3]], ",")[[1]]
tag <- args[[4]]

dir.create(file.path(root, "results", tag), recursive = TRUE, showWarnings = FALSE)

for (round in seq_len(rounds)) {
  shift <- (round - 1L) %% length(variants)
  order <- variants[(seq_along(variants) + shift - 1L) %% length(variants) + 1L]
  for (variant in order) {
    out <- file.path(root, "results", tag, sprintf("%s-%02d.rds", variant, round))
    status <- system2(
      "Rscript",
      c(
        file.path(root, "bench", "run-one.R"),
        file.path(root, "lib", variant),
        variant,
        out
      )
    )
    if (status != 0L) {
      stop("Failed on ", variant, " round ", round)
    }
    cat("round", round, variant, "done\n")
  }
}

Write /private/tmp/claude-502/-Users-davis-files-r-packages-rray4/f7982187-88c2-42f0-9599-3410a6463… 
args <- commandArgs(trailingOnly = TRUE)
root <- args[[1]]
tag <- args[[2]]
variants <- strsplit(args[[3]], ",")[[1]]

files <- list.files(file.path(root, "results", tag), full.names = TRUE)
results <- lapply(files, readRDS)
labels <- vapply(results, \(x) x$label, character(1))

hashes <- lapply(results, \(x) x$hashes)
stopifnot(all(vapply(hashes, \(x) identical(x, hashes[[1]]), logical(1))))
cat("All outputs identical across", length(results), "runs\n\n")

cases <- names(results[[1]]$times)

medians <- sapply(variants, function(variant) {
  times <- do.call(cbind, lapply(results[labels == variant], \(x) x$times))
  apply(times, 1, median)
})

spreads <- sapply(variants, function(variant) {
  times <- do.call(cbind, lapply(results[labels == variant], \(x) x$times))
  apply(times, 1, \(x) (max(x) - min(x)) / median(x))
})

table <- data.frame(case = cases, round(medians, 2), check.names = FALSE)
ratios <- data.frame(
  case = cases,
  round(medians / medians[, variants[[1]]], 3),
  check.names = FALSE
)

options(width = 200)
cat("Median of per-round medians (ms, tiny cases in us)\n")
print(table, row.names = FALSE)
cat("\nRatio to", variants[[1]], "\n")
print(ratios, row.names = FALSE)
cat("\nMax spread across rounds (max - min) / median\n")
print(data.frame(case = cases, round(spreads, 3), check.names = FALSE), row.names = FALSE)

A single round is too noisy to trust—raw [4000, 1] and [1, 4000] results swung wildly, likely from allocation/page-fault noise rather than a real difference. I'm now running 10 rounds of all 7 versions in the background (~12 minutes), rotating order each round for a cleaner signal.

#define RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP(CTYPE, POKE)                                       \
  for (; !rray_run_iterator_done(&it); rray_run_iterator_next(&it, 1)) {       \
    const r_ssize start = rray_run_iterator_start(&it);                        \
    const r_ssize end = rray_run_iterator_end(&it);                            \
                                                                               \
    r_ssize x_loc = rray_run_iterator_loc(&it, 0);                             \
    const r_ssize x_stride = rray_run_iterator_stride(&it, 0);                 \
                                                                               \
    if (x_stride == 0) {                                                       \
      CTYPE const x_elt = v_x[x_loc];                                          \
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
        POKE(out, i, x_elt);                                                   \
      }                                                                        \
    } else {                                                                   \
      for (r_ssize i = start; i < end; ++i) {                                  \
        POKE(out, i, v_x[x_loc]);                                              \
        x_loc += x_stride;                                                     \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
  }

#define RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC_POKE(OUT, I, VALUE) v_out[I] = (VALUE)

35 more lines cut

Thinking

The diff checks out—out is the only new allocation in each kernel and it's protected immediately, with the iterator built beforehand so no unprotected object can leak on error. I'll wait for the benchmark to finish.

Monitor

The benchmark is running in the background: 10 rounds of 7 versions, about 12 minutes in total. I'll post the results when it finishes.

So far:

  • The merged iterator (one struct, no plan) is on the branch and passes all 3497 tests. Nothing is committed.

  • In the disassembly, the element loops are the same as before. The raw fixed-stride run is still a memset call, and the stride-1 run is still vectorized.

  • The merged version does read a few more fields back from the stack between runs, and it adds a stack probe to every call for its 32 KB frame. Those two costs are what the short-run and tiny-array cases are there to measure.

Claude

Thinking

The results confirm raw short runs regress 8.5-12% across merged versions due to per-run reloads, while extern_ptr and inline8 are ruled out as fixes. Since clang keeps the constructor separate in every merged version, I'll now test forcing it inline with always_inline, trying both kernels-inlined and kernels-noinline variants to see if that resolves the regression.

Shell cwd was reset to ~/files/r/packages/rray4

I don't think the merged struct is ready yet. It matches the current design everywhere except one case: it's consistently 9% to 13% slower on raw broadcasts with very short runs. That held in every merged version I tried. The change is on the branch, uncommitted, and passes all 3497 tests.

How I measured

  • I built each version into its own library and ran each round in a fresh R session. Each case is the median of 25 iterations (20,000 for the tiny ones). Rounds rotate the order of the versions, and the tables use the median across rounds.

  • I checked the output of every case in every run, and they were all identical.

  • Hardware: M2 Pro, Apple clang 17, -O2.

  • Full run: 10 rounds × 7 versions × 31 cases (double, integer and raw on 9 shapes, character on 2, plus 2 tiny arrays).

  • Focused rerun: 10 rounds × 6 versions on the short-run and tiny cases.

The versions

  • base: the last commit, with a separate plan and iterator.

  • inline: this branch, one struct with a static inline constructor.

  • extern: one struct, with the constructor as a normal function that returns by value.

  • extern_ptr: one struct, with a normal constructor that fills a struct rray_run_iterator*.

  • inline8: like inline, but with RRAY_MAX_INPUTS set to 8, so the struct is 4 KB instead of 32 KB.

  • ai: like inline, with the constructor forced inline (always_inline).

  • _nk (on base, inline and ai): the kernels are marked noinline, to match what happens with function-pointer dispatch in binary and reduce.

What I found

  • Double, integer and character: every version is within noise of base. That includes the short-run cases and the 20-axis case.

  • Raw with short runs: this is the regression.

    Case base base_nk inline ai inline_nk ai_nk
    raw [2, 1] to [2, 5e6] 9.58 9.58 10.46 10.48 10.40 10.45
    raw [2, 1] × 10 to [2] × 20 1.35 1.36 1.54 1.53 1.53 1.53

    Times are in ms, and the spread for these rows is only 4% to 12%. base_nk matching base shows the slowdown comes from the struct itself, not from inlining. Raw [10, 1] to [10, 1e6] is 4% to 9% slower too, but the spread there is too wide to be sure.

  • Raw with large outputs: too noisy to read (spreads of 100% to 200%, probably page faults). The medians match.

  • Tiny arrays: no per-call cost (0.74 µs either way), so the 32 KB frame doesn't matter.

  • extern_ptr: 60% slower on tiny arrays (0.74 to 1.19 µs), and 17% slower on integer [200, 1, 200]. That rules it out.

  • inline8: no better than inline, so the struct's size isn't the cause. That also takes care of my worry about the earlier unexplained slowdown when copying the struct.

Why it's slower

Inside a run the loops are identical. The fixed stride is still a memset call, and stride 1 is still vectorized. The cost is in the step from one run to the next. With two structs, clang keeps start and the location in registers. With one struct, it reads start, end, size, the location and the stride back from the stack on every run.

My guess is that v_point[axis], v_dimensions[axis] and v_strides[axis * n] are indexed with a value only known at run time. That stops clang from keeping any field of the struct in a register, including the fixed-position ones. When those arrays were in the separate plan, the small iterator didn't have this problem. Forcing the constructor inline doesn't change it, and it nearly doubles the size of each kernel.

The cost only shows up on raw because a 1-byte copy does so little work per run. Binary and reduce kernels do more work per element, so I'd expect the same cost to be smaller there, but I haven't measured that.

Options

  1. Keep two structs, and revert the branch to b81475a. This is what I'd do. The accessors from earlier already hide the plan from call sites, so the only cost of two structs is one extra argument.

  2. Accept the 9% to 13% on short raw runs. In absolute terms it's about 0.9 ms on a 10-million-element result.

  3. Keep trying to get one struct to stay in registers. I don't have a fix ready, and it would mean working against clang's optimizer, which may change between compiler versions.

Loose ends

  • The header comment "The plan holds only immutable state..." in src/strided-iterator2.h is wrong under the merged design. I haven't touched it, since comments are yours to write. It only matters if we keep option 2.

  • The 8 worktrees and benchmark scripts are in the scratchpad. The scripts can be reused for the binary and reduce work. The worktrees are registered with the repo, so I'll remove them once you've picked an option.

Which option do you want?

Davis

Option 2. Seems totally acceptable to me

The merged iterator stays on the branch. I've removed the 8 scratch worktrees, so only the main checkout is left. The benchmark scripts are still in the scratchpad.

The change is uncommitted in 4 files:

  • src/strided-iterator2.h: struct rray_run_plan is gone. struct rray_run_iterator now holds the dimensions, the strides of all inputs and dimensionality. Its constructor takes (v_dimensions, dimensionality, v_strides, n) and does the coalescing itself, and rray_run_iterator_next() now takes only (it, n). I added rray_run_iterator_size(), rray_run_iterator_stride() now reads row 0 of v_strides, and the coalescing helpers are renamed to rray__run_iterator_axes_*.

  • src/decl/strided-iterator2-decl.h: the same renames.

  • src/broadcast.c: the driver passes v_dimensions, dimensionality and v_x_broadcast_strides to each typed kernel. RRAY_BROADCAST_ATOMIC() and RRAY_BROADCAST_BARRIER() build the iterator before allocating out, and RRAY_BROADCAST_LOOP() walks it.

  • src/decl/broadcast-decl.h: the new kernel signatures.

All 3497 tests pass, and clang-format has been run over src/.

The header comment in src/strided-iterator2.h still says "The plan holds only immutable state. The positions that change as it walks are managed by the caller." That no longer matches the code. I've left it alone because comments are yours to write, but you'll probably want to change it before committing.

#include "arithmetic.h"

#include "broadcast-names.h"
#include "dimensionality.h"
#include "dimensions.h"
#include "type.h"
#include "utils.h"

r_obj* rray_binary_arithmetic(
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* y,
  rray_binary_arithmetic_switch_fn fn_switch,
  struct rray_arg* x_arg,
  struct rray_arg* y_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  check_unclassed(x, x_arg, error_call);
  check_unclassed(y, y_arg, error_call);

  x = KEEP(arg_as_array(x, x_arg, error_call));
  y = KEEP(arg_as_array(y, y_arg, error_call));

  const rray_binary_arithmetic_fn fn =
    fn_switch(x, y, x_arg, y_arg, error_call);

  r_obj* x_dimensions = r_dim(x);
  r_obj* y_dimensions = r_dim(y);

  const int* v_x_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(x_dimensions);
  const int* v_y_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(y_dimensions);

  const int x_dimensionality =
    rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(x_dimensions);
  const int y_dimensionality =
    rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(y_dimensions);

  r_obj* dimensions = KEEP(rray_dimensions2(
    v_x_dimensions,
    x_dimensionality,
    v_y_dimensions,
    y_dimensionality,
    x_arg,
    y_arg,
    error_call
  ));
  const int* v_dimensions = r_int_cbegin(dimensions);
  const int dimensionality = rray_dimensionality_from_dimensions(dimensions);

  struct rray_strided_iterator2_plan plan = rray_broadcast_iterator2_plan(
    v_x_dimensions,
    x_dimensionality,
    v_y_dimensions,
    y_dimensionality,
    v_dimensions,
    dimensionality
  );

  r_obj* out = KEEP(fn(x, y, &plan, error_call));
  r_attrib_poke_dim(out, dimensions);

  r_obj* out_names = KEEP(rray_broadcast_names2(x, y, dimensions));

  if (out_names != r_null) {
    r_attrib_poke_dim_names(out, out_names);
  }

  FREE(5);
  return out;
}

r_no_return void stop_unsupported_arithmetic(
  const char* op,
  r_obj* x,
  r_obj* y,
  struct rray_arg* x_arg,
  struct rray_arg* y_arg,
  struct r_lazy error_call
) {
  r_abort_lazy_call(
    error_call,
    "Can't apply `%s` to %s and %s.",
    op,
    rray_arg_type_format(x_arg, rray_typeof(x)),
    rray_arg_type_format(y_arg, rray_typeof(y))
  );
}

r_no_return void stop_int_overflow(struct r_lazy error_call) {
  r_abort_lazy_call(error_call, "Integer overflow.");
}
#ifndef RRAY_BINARY_H
#define RRAY_BINARY_H

#include "rlang.h"

#include "strided-iterator.h"

#define RRAY_BINARY_ARGS(...) , __VA_ARGS__
#define RRAY_BINARY_NO_ARGS

#define RRAY_BINARY(                                                           \
  X_CTYPE,                                                                     \
  X_CONST_DEREF,                                                               \
  X_CAST,                                                                      \
  Y_CTYPE,                                                                     \
  Y_CONST_DEREF,                                                               \
  Y_CAST,                                                                      \
  OUT_RTYPE,                                                                   \
  OUT_CTYPE,                                                                   \
  OUT_DEREF,                                                                   \
  ONE,                                                                         \
  ONE_ARGS                                                                     \
)                                                                              \
  const r_ssize size = rray_strided_iterator2_plan_size(plan);                 \
                                                                               \
  r_obj* out = KEEP(r_alloc_vector(OUT_RTYPE, size));                          \
  OUT_CTYPE* v_out = OUT_DEREF(out);                                           \
                                                                               \
  const X_CTYPE* v_x = X_CONST_DEREF(x);                                       \
  const Y_CTYPE* v_y = Y_CONST_DEREF(y);                                       \
                                                                               \
  r_ssize run_start = 0;                                                       \
  const r_ssize run_size = rray_strided_iterator2_plan_run_size(plan);         \
                                                                               \
  r_ssize x_start = 0;                                                         \
  const r_ssize x_run_stride = rray_strided_iterator2_plan_run_stride1(plan);  \
                                                                               \
  r_ssize y_start = 0;                                                         \
  const r_ssize y_run_stride = rray_strided_iterator2_plan_run_stride2(plan);  \
                                                                               \
  r_ssize v_point[RRAY_MAX_DIMENSIONALITY];                                    \
  rray_strided_iterator2_plan_point_init(plan, v_point);                       \
                                                                               \
  while (run_start != size) {                                                  \
    const r_ssize run_end = run_start + run_size;                              \
    r_ssize x_loc = x_start;                                                   \
    r_ssize y_loc = y_start;                                                   \
                                                                               \
    if (x_run_stride == 0) {                                                   \
      const X_CTYPE x_elt = v_x[x_loc];                                        \
      if (y_run_stride == 0) {                                                 \
        const Y_CTYPE y_elt = v_y[y_loc];                                      \
        for (r_ssize i = run_start; i < run_end; ++i) {                        \
          v_out[i] = ONE(X_CAST(x_elt), Y_CAST(y_elt) ONE_ARGS);               \
        }                                                                      \
      } else {                                                                 \
        for (r_ssize i = run_start; i < run_end; ++i) {                        \
          const Y_CTYPE y_elt = v_y[y_loc];                                    \
          v_out[i] = ONE(X_CAST(x_elt), Y_CAST(y_elt) ONE_ARGS);               \
          y_loc += y_run_stride;                                               \
        }                                                                      \
      }                                                                        \
    } else if (y_run_stride == 0) {                                            \
      const Y_CTYPE y_elt = v_y[y_loc];                                        \
      for (r_ssize i = run_start; i < run_end; ++i) {                          \
        const X_CTYPE x_elt = v_x[x_loc];                                      \
        v_out[i] = ONE(X_CAST(x_elt), Y_CAST(y_elt) ONE_ARGS);                 \
        x_loc += x_run_stride;                                                 \
      }                                                                        \
    } else {                                                                   \
      for (r_ssize i = run_start; i < run_end; ++i) {                          \
        const X_CTYPE x_elt = v_x[x_loc];                                      \
        const Y_CTYPE y_elt = v_y[y_loc];                                      \
        v_out[i] = ONE(X_CAST(x_elt), Y_CAST(y_elt) ONE_ARGS);                 \
        x_loc += x_run_stride;                                                 \
        y_loc += y_run_stride;                                                 \
      }                                                                        \
    }                                                                          \
                                                                               \
    run_start = run_end;                                                       \
    RRAY_STRIDED_ITERATOR_NEXT2(x_start, y_start, v_point, plan);              \
  }                                                                            \
                                                                               \
  FREE(1);                                                                     \
  return out;

#ifndef RRAY_REDUCE_H

#include "rlang.h"
